**One correction worth recording, because it changes who owns the defect.** This is **not** a drag2 regression. `packages/drag/src/sortable.stories.tsx` carries the same helper; the burden was inherited from the shipped package and carried through the contract without being examined. The freeze was premature for this, but the rewrite did not introduce it.

**No replacement API is assumed here.** This is a design problem, and the probe's job is to state it as a case and enumerate candidates — not to pick one. Any candidate has to be priced against what the current shape buys, which 05 is explicit about: the two gates are **independent**, and a resolution that returns a promise instead of awaiting one is what lets the authored re-render overlap the landing animation rather than serialize behind it. A design that loses that overlap is not a simplification.

**A second case belongs here (ledger L-6).** Settle-time `landingTiming()` is the one shipped capability drag2 currently cannot express: `landing()` fixes timing at construction, and two shipped behaviors read the timing _after_ a settlement step has run — rejected sortable timing is read after the placeholder returns home, and free drag's after the home target resolves. It is a settlement question, it is executable, and it is cheapest to carry into Phase 14 alongside the obligation set rather than to rediscover in Phase 15.

**Deliverable.** Both cases stated executably; candidate designs for the authored-presentation protocol with the overlap property and the failure-visibility property evaluated against each.

### 13c — A second behavior against the current SPI

**Scope.** Free drag written as a **typed probe** — every seam implemented, every driver typed, `@ts-expect-error` on each claim that is expressible — with inert stubs where a lifecycle would be needed. Explicitly the `contract.ts` treatment, and explicitly _not_ an implementation: at 2,300 lines that fixture could look executable, and 00 already warns that typecheck cannot catch a lifecycle error in one.

**What it is probing.** The places where free drag is shaped differently from a sortable, each of which is a candidate SPI gap:

| Free-drag requirement | The question it puts to the SPI |
| --- | --- |
| Clamp to `bounds` before writing | 00 claims P-2 is resolved at no hot-path cost. One behavior has tested that claim; this is the one it was written for. |
| `coordinateSpace` mapping | Where does a consumer coordinate space live — behavior, feature, or kernel? The sortable never needed one. |
| Three lift modes as a **public option** | drag2's `LIFT_FAITHFUL`/`LIFT_FLAT`/`LIFT_IN_PLACE` are kernel-internal constants and are **not** a renaming of the shipped `top-layer`/`flatten`/`none`. Phase 11 found the hard way that lift mode is behavioral, not cosmetic. Making it public is a surface decision, not a mapping. |
| `controller.update()` with live policy and a controlled `position` | 00 says P-1 is resolved — "policy updates are behavior actions and the kernel supplies the transition envelope". Never exercised. A _controlled position_ is stronger: it is externally driven motion, which the kernel's pointer-owned ingress has no route for. |
| `resolveHomeTarget` / `FreeHomeTarget` | A synchronous rollback target, against the sortable's placeholder-anchored home. Does `anchorTarget` cover it? |
| `onMove(geometry)` per sample | A per-sample consumer callback on the hot path M-1 measured without one. |

**Deliverable.** A write-up naming each requirement the frozen SPI cannot express, and each one it can — the second list is as valuable as the first.

**Gate.** Phase 14 does not start until all three probes exist. A prose-only finding remains insufficient (00) **for an SPI change**, and that rule is not relaxed for the owner.

**Read precisely, because Revision 2 later did something this sentence has been mistaken for forbidding.** The rule fences the SPI: seams, ownership, frame rules, lifecycle — the construction model frozen so an implementation probe could be run against a fixed target. It has never fenced the **public consumer API**, and it could not, because the consumer API is the thing the library exists to decide. Revision 2 changed the consumer surface on the strength of four probes for the decisions that touch mechanism (D-36…D-39, D-42, D-43, D-46) and on owner judgement alone for the five that do not (D-40, D-41, D-44, D-45, D-47), and 00 §Status states which is which rather than dressing the second group in evidence it does not have. **"Not relaxed for the owner" means the owner may not wave an SPI change through on prose. It does not mean the owner may not decide what a consumer writes.**

---

## Phase 14 — The single contract revision

**Contract revision complete — D-32…D-35, dated 2026-08-04.** Documents 00–06 revised in place, in one pass. Implementation is **not** part of this phase: the SPI-level code changes land with their consumers (Phase 15 for D-33, Phase 16 for D-32) or with the second behavior (Phases 19–20 for D-34, D-35), and Checkpoint C reviews the revision before any of it is built on. **This booking is what F-63 caught** — a decision stated in the present tense with a landing date and no instrument between the two — and D-76 replaced it: D-34 and D-35 landed **2026-08-15**, as their own step before Phase 19, against the sortable alone.

| # | Change | Decision |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 13a | Lifecycle-intent vocabulary | **D-32 — a second admission member**, `command: { types, admit(event, draft) }`, not an intent protocol. No `KernelHost` member; no new phase; no new frame field. `PENDING` redefined as _activation not yet committed_; `pointerId === -1` made normative for a pointerless operation. |
| 13b | Authored-presentation protocol | ~~**D-33 — candidate C-3.**~~ **Retracted wholesale by D-41 (Revision 2): the protocol is deleted, not amended.** The resolution declared (`accept({ presentation: true })`), the controller acknowledged (`controller.ready(request)`), and the **request** was the per-operation identity; no settlement primitive reached the consumer, and it was revised at Checkpoint C after C-2 — a kernel-minted token — failed on synchronous commits. All of that was sound and its **premise is gone**: D-33 solved how a consumer acknowledges a render that happens _after_ the resolution returns, and the serial commit removes that window by having the resolution not return until the render is done. A consumer that must render first `await`s its own commit inside `onReorder`. |
| 13c N-1 | Activation staged type | **D-34 — parameterized**: `BehaviorSpec<Part, Activation extends {} = true>`. |
| 13c N-2 | The visual's rendered delta | **D-35 — no seam.** The lift session records what `write(x, y)` rendered and the kernel reads its own object. |

**Two probe results were declined as Phase 14 scope, as the probes asked:** settle-time landing timing (13b B-2 — ergonomics, Phase 15 or 22) and public lift modes / coordinate-space ownership (13c P-2, P-4 — Phase 18).

**Checkpoint C took five passes plus a follow-up round, and is closed. The record of what each one found is the point of the checkpoint** — and the shape of that record is itself a result: pass 1 reopened a decision, passes 2 and 3 closed contradictions, pass 4 changed no decision while finding two blockers _underneath_ all four of them, pass 5 found two more of the same kind one level down, and the C6 follow-ups found three in pass 5's own corrections. **No decision was reopened after pass 1.**

Read the shape rather than the count. Every pass after the first fixed something true at the level it was written and unfinished one level down — the prose was corrected and the type, the ordering, or the obligation the change created was not. D-35's interval was narrowed while `dispose()` stayed on the behavior's capability; claim-before-dispatch was stated and applied to one of its two sites; the fixture began publishing runtime state without the clearing obligation and failure path that publication implies. That is the useful artefact here, more than the four decisions: **a correction is not finished when its sentence is true.**

| Pass | Review | Outcome |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | [`checkpoint-c-1.md`](reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-1.md) | Not closed. Two blockers against D-33 (C-01 synchronous-commit ordering, C-02 incoherent `abandon()`), five consistency findings. D-32, D-34, D-35 accepted on direction. **D-33 reopened and replaced** — the kernel-minted token became a controller acknowledgement keyed on request identity. |
| 2 | [`checkpoint-c-2.md`](reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-2.md) | Not closed. D-33's _direction_ accepted; four items — an overclaim about the opt-in declaration (C2-01), the request-identity data path not shown normatively (C2-02), token-era residue in trace 06 (C2-03), and the fixture missing from the reviewed change set (C2-04). All four addressed: the declaration stays opt-in with the claims corrected to match; the identity path is normative in 01, 02, 06 and compiled in the fixture; 06 carries only the C-3 protocol; the fixture is staged. |
| 3 | [`checkpoint-c-3.md`](reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-3.md) | **No decision changed.** Two documents carried two normative readings of one event, and both are now single-valued: an acknowledgement contradicting its own resolution is _reported and discarded_ (C3-01), and `rt.pendingRequest` publishes **after** the committed render (C3-04). Both report paths are visible — the behavior's as a call, the kernel's as the seal rule (C3-02) — and the fixture's stale line-count claim is gone (C3-03). |
| 4 | [`checkpoint-c-4.md`](reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-4.md) | **Two blockers, no decision reopened — and the pass that closed the checkpoint.** The D-32 command lost its destination on the very lifecycle it reuses (C4-01), and D-35's sole-writer rule was neither true for the interval it named nor tier B (C4-02). Six further findings: the construction surface bypassed opaque `Behavior` (C4-03), the main seal algorithm omitted C3's contradiction branch (C4-04), three top-precedence ledger rows still described the pre-revision model (C4-05), the fixture described ownership paths it did not instantiate (C4-06), matrix and trace gaps (C4-07), and broken review provenance with three wrong counts (C4-08). All eight corrected; two follow-up gaps found in the correction itself — `release.effect`'s pointerless branch, and the duplicate acknowledgement in the armed window's dispatch-to-drain interior — then two textual drifts. |
| 5 | [`checkpoint-c-5.md`](reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-5.md) | **Two blockers, both in pass 4's own corrections, no decision reopened.** C4-02's D-35 fix corrected the interval and the tier but still handed the behavior `dispose()` on the kernel-owned lift session — I-34 breakable through a first-class method rather than the tier-C escape hatch (C5-01). C4-04's claim-before-dispatch rule was applied to the live armed path but **not** to arm's dispatch of the copied early latch, which reopened the duplicate-release race across the early-to-armed boundary (C5-02). Plus a matrix assertion that faithful lift mode cannot satisfy (C5-03), a fixture whose pointerless body told a different lifecycle story than 02 (C5-04), and four editorial items (C5-05). **Three follow-ups then surfaced in pass 5's own corrections and are tagged C6-01…C6-03 in the contract** — no separate review file: the projected `write` is retained and stays effective, so the blanket late-use-safe claim was too wide and a _temporal_ tier-C rule was needed (C6-01); publishing `rt.placeholder`/`rt.lift` created a clearing obligation `retire()` did not discharge and a failure path that returned normally, leaving the staged command executable (C6-02); and the cross-window duplicate row named a table position instead of the arrival rule (C6-03). |

The revision is backed by a compiled fixture, `packages/drag2/tests/revision/phase-14.ts`, which is what pass 2 onward has that pass 1 did not.

**Scope.** Revise contract documents 00–06 **once**, against all three probes together, and re-freeze.

**Deliverables.**

- The lifecycle-intent vocabulary from 13a, or a recorded decision not to add one.
- The settlement / authored-presentation protocol from 13b — one of the five enumerated candidates, or a recorded decision to keep the current shape.
- **13c N-1** — the activation seam's staged type, parameterized or deliberately left pinned to `HTMLElement`.
- **13c N-2** — how the kernel learns the visual's rendered delta, and what that costs on the hot path if the sortable has to answer it too.
- A record of what 13c proved _sufficient_, since an unchanged seam validated by a second behavior is a stronger claim than an unexamined one: the six `P-*` rows are that record and belong in the revised 02.
- The 05 test matrix extended with the rows the revision creates.
- **Re-verification**: the whole existing suite green against the revised SPI, with every deviation Part I recorded re-checked rather than assumed.

**Done when.** The contract set is internally consistent at the new SPI, and no probe case from Phase 13 is left unaddressed — addressed includes "declined, with a reason".

**What re-freezing does and does not mean.** It restores 00's admissible-change rule; it does not close the contract. A later phase that produces a failing executable case the SPI cannot express reopens it, and that is the system working. See §Ordering principle.

**Deviations and decisions recorded while revising.**

- **The four changes were not independent, and two of them were decided differently because of it.** Written up in 02 §Where the four changes touch each other, which is the single-revision rule earning its keep rather than a formality. D-32 needed D-35 to be _correct_: a pointerless operation would otherwise have computed its landing origin from a `-1` sentinel and two zeroes, and would have shipped a landing that opens off-screen. D-32 shrank to one member because the open draft already carries what 13a's candidate wanted to stage — threading a `Prepared` from a native listener to a queued release seam would have broken 02 §The staged value never outlives its transaction _and_ forced a second staged type parameter beside D-34's. D-33 is the only change that touches nothing else, and that is stated rather than assumed.
- **D-35 replaced 13c's own suggested fix.** The probe proposed a `renderedDelta(current): Point` seam; the revision records the delta inside `VisualLiftSession.write` instead. It adds two scalar field writes and no seam — _not_ "nothing", which is what this bullet said until C4-02, and the writes are unmeasured until Phase 21 — tracks writes that do not come from `moved` (a controlled position writes from an `action.effect`), and no behavior implements anything. 13c's N-2 assertion therefore **still fails to compile after the revision**, deliberately: no seam reports the rendered delta because none needs to. The probe's annotation should say so — a Phase 15 hygiene item, not a contract one.
- **The contract said `lift.composeXY` where the code has said `lift.write` since phase 6.** D-35's sole-writer rule depends on which one is normative, so the prose was corrected across 02, 05 and 06 rather than left as a harmless drift. The hot-path accounting is unchanged: three post-`MOVE` indirect calls, `spec.moved` · `lift.write` · `frame.schedule`.
- **Two Part I deviations were ratified into the normative `BehaviorSpec` listing**: `config.actionTags` and `reportFailure`. Both have existed since phases 4–5 and were described in 02's prose while missing from its listing. `reportFailure` is load-bearing for D-32 — a throwing `command.admit` has exactly the Q-1 shape a throwing `admit` has — so the listing could not stay silent about it.
- **D-33 was decided twice, and the first answer was wrong — and at Revision 2 it is deleted, which makes it three times.** D-41 removes `controller.ready(request)`, `ResolutionOptions`, `KernelHost.presentationCommitted()`, `rt.pendingRequest`, `authoredReady`, the acknowledgement deadline and the readiness-specific retarget lifecycle, and narrows settlement to **one** gate. **The whole of the bullet below is retained deliberately**, because it is the corpus's best record of why per-operation identity on a controller method is hard — a problem D-47's kernel surface will meet again — and because the second answer failed for a reason the third answer makes structural: _a capability minted by the settlement is younger than the render it acknowledges_. The serial order's contribution is not a better identity but the removal of the interval in which identity was needed. **The lesson, stated once so it is not re-derived: two of the three answers were about who holds the identity, and the one that worked was about deleting the window.** Original bullet follows. Phase 14 first chose 13b's candidate C-2 — a kernel-minted `PresentationToken` delivered while the settlement armed — because it inverts _creation_, which is where three of the four consumer obligations came from. Checkpoint C found two blockers, and both trace to one root: **a capability minted by the settlement is younger than the render it acknowledges.** Under `flushSync`, a synchronous renderer, or any consumer that commits inside `onReorder`, the layout effect runs before the token exists, acknowledges nothing, and the gate times out (C-01). And the `abandon()` state that design needed — release the gate without failing — let an accepted settlement report `onFinish` over an authored DOM still showing the old order (C-02).

  The replacement is candidate **C-3**, which 13b rejected for a reason that turned out to be fixable: a controller method appears to have no per-operation identity. It does not need to _invent_ one. The **request** is public, per-operation, and in the consumer's hand _before_ the render, because it is the argument to the callback that asked for the render. Keying `controller.ready(request)` on it closes the stale window — A times out, A retires, B reaches its own resolution, A's late effect fires and is rejected — with no new type, no generation counter and no token. The behavior compares by identity against the request it published from `release.effect`; the kernel never learns what a request is.

  Net effect on the public surface: **one added alias** (`ResolutionOptions`) and one controller method, instead of two exported types describing a kernel-owned gate. `abandon()` does not exist — see the next entry.

- **The presentation declaration stays opt-in, and the claims moved instead** (C2-01). The follow-up review is right that `presentation?: boolean` defaulting to _no hold_ keeps probe 13b's R-2 shape alive, and that an earlier draft claimed the consumer "cannot omit the hold" when it plainly can. Of the two directions offered, **B — keep the default, state the discipline honestly** — is the correct one: flipping it converts the _legitimate_ imperative consumer, the one 02 §`authoredReady` and the shipped package both endorse, into a 500 ms stall and a classified failure. That trades a silent error for a loud wrong one on the simplest correct call site there is.

  What the pass added instead of a default flip is the observation that **three of the four consumer error modes are loud**, not two: declaring without acknowledging hits the deadline, and _acknowledging without declaring_ is a **declared contradiction** the kernel can report without inferring anything from DOM mutation — so it does not touch the rule that acceptance is never inferred. The undetectable residue narrows to a consumer that used neither half of the protocol, which is indistinguishable from one that renders synchronously and is tier C. I-35, F-46, F-6 and 03's public documentation are all reworded against that table.

- **A state was deleted rather than repaired.** C-02 offered four coherent meanings for `abandon()`; the closest is "not legal for an accepted destination outcome", and a state that is illegal in the only case anyone would reach for it should not exist. Readiness now has exactly three outcomes: acknowledged, the deadline, or retirement. A consumer whose render failed has left an accepted reorder unrendered — the operation genuinely did not complete, and the deadline is the honest terminal. What is lost against `presentationReady`'s rejection channel is **latency only**, and `readinessTimeout` is a public option. If Phase 15 shows it matters, the smallest addition is a second argument to `ready()` carrying an error; recorded, not built.
- **`preventDefault()` moved to the kernel** (C-03), for `admit` as well as `command.admit`. The behavior answers feasibility with its return value; the ingress owner performs the browser effect. An earlier draft left the call to the behavior _and_ rated I-32 tier A, which a member holding the real `Event` can trivially violate — two errors compounding. I-32 is now tier **B with a stated tier-C residue**, which is what the mechanism actually delivers. **Ownership survives D-46 unchanged; the _policy_ does not.** The kernel is still the party that calls it, and D-46 changes when: the pointer path stops calling it unconditionally at admission, and both paths gate on an admission that asks what the event landed on. One party responsible, a narrower condition.
- **One capability is admitted as not expressible**, rather than reserved for: a multi-press keyboard drag — pick up, move, drop across several key events — needs a producer of a release the kernel does not own. D-32 mints a one-slot command, which is the shipped semantics and the ledger's retained behavior. New question Q-13 in 05, and a row in 00 §What would falsify this model. Phase 16's accessibility review is where the case would come from.
- **The public surface takes one breaking change**, and only one: `ReorderResolution.accept/reject` take `ResolutionOptions` instead of a `presentationReady` promise, `SortableController` gains `ready(request)`, and `ResolutionOptions` joins `sortable/callbacks.js`. That is **one** added alias; the token design would have added two types describing a kernel-owned gate, and Checkpoint C's "do not expose more settlement machinery than the consumer needs" is why it does not. Recorded in 03 §The export topology this requires and §The public/internal boundary. `CommandAdmission` stays **internal** — a behavior declares which events the kernel binds; a consumer does not.
- **A stale number was repeated as evidence, and is corrected.** The first pass wrote "the behavior tag count is still two" as proof that nothing had grown. The implementation has declared `config.actionTags: 3` since Checkpoint B — `TAG_INVALIDATION` carries a failure raised from a native scroll/resize listener back into a seam, because a seam is the only place a stage can be classified — and a Checkpoint B review flagged that the contract never recorded it. Corrected in 02 §`ActionTransition` and in 05 §Q-4, with the investigation Q-4 asks for: the third tag is not a lifecycle request in disguise, and the pressure that motivated Q-4 did not become a tag at all.
- **The request-identity path is normative, and `ResolutionCommand` did not have to change** (C2-02). **Deleted by D-41 (Revision 2) along with the protocol it served** — `rt.pendingRequest` goes, `SortableRuntime` returns to six mutable fields, and `controller.ready` has nothing to compare. **The half worth keeping is the restraint**: this bullet's conclusion was that a sortable domain value must not be put onto a kernel SPI type for one behavior's identity need, and that holds independently of whether the identity need exists. Original bullet follows. The follow-up review asked whether the exact request forces a field onto the staged command, and flagged it as a legitimate contract correction if so. It does not: `release.prepare` writes `proposal` into the draft _before_ returning the command, so `release.effect` reaches the same object through `current.proposal` that the `invoke` closure captured. Putting a sortable domain value onto a kernel SPI type for one behavior's identity need would be the exact mistake D-34 and D-35 were opened to correct.

  What did change: `SortableRuntime` gains `pendingRequest`, bringing it to **seven** mutable fields beside three readonly ones — this said "an eighth mutable field" until C4-08, counting the readonly three among them. It is published by `release.effect`, compared by `===` in `controller.ready`, cleared by `retire()`. Written out in 02 §The identity path, in full; threaded through 01's runtime listing and ownership table, 02's seam table, 06's trace and retirement, and the fixture. **Typecheck cannot prove object identity** — a structurally equal literal is assignable — so the fixture shows the mechanism and 05's matrix carries the row that pins it.

- **A compiled fixture now backs the revision** (C-06): `packages/drag2/tests/revision/phase-14.ts`, type-only, picked up by the package's existing `docs/**/*` include. It restates only what the revision changed and imports the rest from `src/`, so the halves cannot drift; it carries two complete `BehaviorSpec`s — sortable with `Activation = HTMLElement`, free drag with the `true` default — the construction handshake, the reference integration, and **eleven** `@ts-expect-error` assertions plus one positive shape check. It said twelve until C4-06; `n12` is a positive seven-field `KernelFrame` assignment, and counting it among the negatives overstated the negative evidence by one. Pass 4 also replaced four `declare`s with compiled paths — `unbrand → factory → arm`, one runtime per install factory, a `LandingContext` whose `from` _is_ `lift.rendered`, and both sortable seams branching on `pointerId` — because a fixture that describes an ownership path proves only that two aliases are compatible. It is not lifecycle validation and says so. The Phase 13 probes are untouched and still import the pre-revision SPI, which is what makes them evidence of what Phase 13 found.
- **An invalid acknowledgement is reported, and the pass that asked for it fixed the last place two readings survived** (C3-01, C3-02). 02 said a latch for an operation that declared no presentation was "simply dropped"; 06 said the same event was "ignored and reported". One event, two normative answers, so the review is right that this had to be chosen rather than reconciled. **Reported and discarded** is now normative everywhere, and three sub-decisions fell out of making it so:

  - **The discard happens at seal**, not at settlement creation. `prepare` returning `{ presentation: true }` does not yet mean a hold exists — taking it is `settlement.effect`'s to do — so seal is the first moment the gate plan is complete. The payoff is that `arm` reads the latch unconditionally: after seal, a set latch always has a hold behind it.
  - **The report is scoped to a _successful_ seal.** If `settlement.effect` threw, the latch dies with every other unarmed request and nothing is reported to the consumer, because that contradiction is the seam's and the queued failure checkpoint already owns it.
  - **The reporting surface was not open, and is not new.** All four invalid acknowledgements — three consumer-side, one kernel-side — take `kernel/reporter.ts` `report`, gated on `DEV`, and none classifies. Gating on `DEV` does not weaken C2-01's position: the audience is the integrator and the F-6 witness, both of which run in `DEV`, and in production the identity check has already made the acknowledgement harmless.

- **`rt.pendingRequest` publishes after the committed render** (C3-04). **Moot after D-41 (Revision 2)** — the field does not exist, so neither ordering does. Kept because the _reason_ one order beat the other is reusable: a throwing `release.effect` classifies `FAILURE_RELEASE` and the staged command is never executed, so publishing state that names a round-trip which cannot happen is the worse of two valid orders. Original bullet follows. The fixture published first and 06 published last; both satisfy D-33, which requires only that publication precede `onReorder`, and both are inside the same effect. Last is the better of two valid orders for the reason the review gives: a throwing `release.effect` classifies `FAILURE_RELEASE` and the staged command is never executed, so publishing first would name a round-trip that cannot happen. Nothing breaks if it does — the kernel has no attempt and no armed hold, so it reports and releases nothing — which is why this is ordering hygiene rather than a defect, and why it was cheap to make uniform.
- **What the revision did not touch, checked rather than assumed:** the kernel frame slice is still seven fields; the phase vocabulary is still eight; the seam set is unchanged apart from D-34's type parameter; and `KernelHost` grew by exactly one member, `presentationCommitted`, which is **D-33's and not D-32's** — a whole second input mode cost the host nothing. Every negative assertion in all three typed probes still fails to compile, which is the property that makes them worth keeping.

**What Phase 14 did not do, and where it goes.** The plan's re-verification deliverable — "the whole existing suite green against the revised SPI, with every deviation Part I recorded re-checked rather than assumed" — belongs with the implementation, because there is nothing to re-run against a document. It is carried into Phase 15 as an explicit obligation and named in Checkpoint C's exit condition below.

---

## Checkpoint C — the contract re-freeze

**Closed 2026-08-04, after five review passes and one follow-up round. The Phase 14 contract re-freeze is complete.** See §Exit below for the fifteen criteria and what the re-freeze does and does not license.

**What to review.** The revision itself, before a line of it is built on.

- Does each change trace to a Phase 13 case, or did scope leak in?
- Is the lifecycle-intent vocabulary the _smallest_ one that works, or has it become a generic behavior-to-kernel protocol — which 02 explicitly declined to reserve? **The revision claims it is a second admission rather than a vocabulary at all** (D-32); the sharpest test of that claim is whether the one-slot rule (§Q-13) is a kernel decision that should have been the behavior's.
- Does the new settlement protocol keep the gate independence and the render/landing overlap 05 relies on? **And is the narrowing deliberate**: the token has no rejection channel, which is a capability `presentationReady` had.
- Does anything in the revision only make sense for the sortable?
- **Is D-35's sole-writer rule true rather than aspirational?** It makes `lift.write` the only rendering entry point **from acquisition until `LandingContext.from` is sampled** — after which the landing runner is the deliberate writer — and everything downstream of `from` depends on that. (This prompt said "between acquisition and the join" until pass 5; that is the formulation pass 4 **rejected** as both wider than the property needed and false against 02's own landing contract, and leaving it here would have had future reviews test the wrong rule. C5-05.)

**Form.** Owner review plus an independent audit, then a closure assessment — the cadence Checkpoints A and B already ran (`reviews/drag2-review-*`).

**Pass 1 — [`reviews/checkpoint-c-1.md`](reviews/checkpoint-c/checkpoint-c-1.md).** Not closed. D-32, D-34 and D-35 accepted on direction; **D-33 reopened** on two blockers (C-01 synchronous-commit ordering, C-02 incoherent `abandon()` semantics) plus five consistency findings: I-32's tier and `preventDefault()` ownership (C-03), D-34 not threaded through the construction generics (C-04), I-35 overclaiming (C-05), no compiled revision fixture (C-06), and a false allocation claim in probe 13c (C-07). All seven addressed; D-33 replaced with the controller-shaped design. The stale action-tag count was found in the same pass and corrected.

**Exit — the criteria pass 1 set, and they are the ones to check.**

1. The chosen readiness protocol has coherent synchronous, stale, timeout and destroy semantics, and **no fourth state**.
2. The revised contract compiles as a complete type surface — `packages/drag2/tests/revision/phase-14.ts`, green under `npx just typecheck`, with every `@ts-expect-error` still used, **and present in the reviewed change set** rather than untracked (pass 2, C2-04).
3. The normative examples obey the protocol's ordering: the identity is stored before the mutation, in 03, in 06 and in the fixture alike.
4. The invariants claim only what the API and kernel enforce — I-32 is B with a stated residue, I-35 no longer claims the consumer cannot lose an acknowledgement **or omit the declaration**.
5. A forgotten declaration is explicitly retained as tier-C discipline, with no stronger claim attached, and the residue named as F-6's test obligation (pass 2, C2-01).
6. `controller.ready(request)` is specified against **one exact published object**, with retirement clearing it, in 01, 02, 06 and the fixture (pass 2, C2-02).
7. Trace 06 contains only the selected C-3 protocol — no token, deliverer or `abandon()` vocabulary, and one consumer update rather than two (pass 2, C2-03).
8. **Every revised path has one executable reading, including the reference behavior's** (pass 4, C4-01). The pointerless lifecycle is specified in **all three** seams that carry it — `activation.prepare` preserves the command's gap, `release.prepare` takes it without spatial re-resolution, and `release.effect` moves the placeholder but performs no lift write — in 02's seam table, 06 and the fixture alike, and Phase 16 keeps the direct proposal-equivalence assertion.
9. **Each invariant is rated at the guarantee the API provides** (pass 4, C4-02, C4-04). I-34 is B over a tier-C rendering rule and its interval ends where `from` is sampled; I-35 states that cross-operation safety is a behavior obligation, not a kernel one; and the duplicate acknowledgement is specified in **all three** windows, including the dispatch-to-drain interior of the armed one, where the latch is claimed at the call site.
10. **The construction surface is the opaque `Behavior`** in 01 as well as in 03 and the fixture, and the fixture compiles `unbrand → factory → arm` rather than declaring around it (pass 4, C4-03, C4-06).
11. **No contradiction survives in a top-precedence document**: the 00 ledger rows D-8, D-9, D-11 and D-35 state the post-revision rule (pass 4, C4-05).

12. **Every kernel-owned capability is kernel-only in the type, not only in the prose** (pass 5, C5-01). The behavior-facing lift is a positive `Pick` of `visual`, `baseTransform`, `compose`, `write`; `rendered` and `dispose` are unreachable from `ActivationScope`, from `moved` and from the behavior runtime, and the fixture asserts both.
13. **`READINESS_SETTLED` is never dispatched without first claiming `readinessSettled`** — on _every_ path, including arm's dispatch of the copied early latch — and post-settlement classification tests the claimed latch before the absent hold (pass 5, C5-02).
14. **Every guarantee is rated at what the API provides, including the temporal ones** (C6-01). `write` is retained and stays effective, so the blanket late-use-safe claim is narrowed to the two capabilities that have it, and the lift is governed by a stated tier-C temporal rule instead — with the no-hot-path-guard choice recorded as a decision rather than an omission.
15. **State the fixture publishes, it also clears and defends** (C6-02): `retire()` drops `placeholder` and `lift` with `pendingRequest`, and the impossible missing-resource branch throws rather than returning, so the staged `ResolutionCommand` cannot execute for an operation whose presentation was never assembled.

**Checkpoint C is closed and the Phase 14 contract re-freeze is complete.** All fifteen criteria are met. The five review passes and the C6-01…C6-03 follow-ups are preserved above as provenance, not as open work; **no further architectural correction pass is required.** From here 00's admissible-change rule applies again, unchanged: a further contract change requires a failing executable case the re-frozen SPI cannot express.

**What the re-freeze does not mean**, in the words 00 uses: re-freezing restores the rule; it does not close the contract. Phases 15, 16 and 19–20 are the first to run the revised SPI against a runtime, and any one of them may produce such a case. The re-freeze also carries an obligation rather than only a permission — `src/` still implements the **pre-revision** SPI, so the green suite is a baseline and not evidence for D-32…D-35. Phase 14's re-verification obligation travels with the implementation: the existing suite green against the revised SPI, with every Part I deviation re-checked rather than assumed, is a Phase 15 done-when.

## Phase 15 — Settlement protocol implementation

**Landed 2026-08-06 — and substantially unbuilt again at Phase R.** D-41 (Revision 2) retracts D-33 wholesale, so `holdForReadiness()`, `host.presentationCommitted()`, the early-acknowledgement latch, `rt.pendingRequest`, `ResolutionOptions`, `controller.ready`, `readinessTimeout` and the whole `acknowledgement.browser.test.ts` group are deleted rather than amended. **Two things this phase produced survive and are the reason it was not wasted.** First, the re-verification obligation it discharged for Phase 14 — the existing suite green against the revised SPI, every Part I deviation re-checked — is a separate deliverable and stands. Second, `createCommitTracker` is gone from the reference integration and stays gone: D-41 reaches the same end state through a plainer route, an `await` inside `onReorder`, so the observable form of probe 13b's finding being closed is unaffected by the protocol underneath it disappearing. **What this phase is evidence for, after Revision 2, is that the consumer's commit barrier was never the library's problem** — two protocols were built to solve it and the third answer was that the serial order makes it the consumer's own `await`. The rest of the section is the record of the second one and reads as it was written.

D-33 is implemented, `src/` agrees with the fixture, and the suite is green at **663 tests** (30 files, plus the type-fixture project). The 05 §Authored-presentation acknowledgement group is `tests/sortable/acknowledgement.browser.test.ts` — 19 tests, driven entirely through the public entrypoint, because both halves of the protocol are public. `createCommitTracker` is gone from `src/sortable.stories.tsx` and from `tests/sortable/react.browser.test.ts`, which is the observable form of probe 13b's finding being closed.

**Scope.** Implement D-33 — declare in the resolution, acknowledge through the controller, identify by request — plus the re-verification Phase 14 could not perform against a document.

**Deliverables.** The kernel and behavior side — `holdForReadiness()`, `host.presentationCommitted()`, the early-acknowledgement latch on the resolution attempt, and `rt.pendingRequest` published by `release.effect` and cleared by `retire()`; the public surface change (`ReorderResolution.accept/reject` take `ResolutionOptions`, `SortableController` gains `ready(request)`, `ResolutionOptions` exports from `sortable/callbacks.js`); the React reference integration updated — `createCommitTracker` should disappear entirely, and the one residual consumer obligation that survives (acknowledge, or eat the deadline) is documented as a deliberate cost rather than left implicit in a story file. `tests/sortable/react.browser.test.ts` and `sortable.stories.tsx` both move with it.

**The fixture is the specification for the type half.** `packages/drag2/tests/revision/phase-14.ts` already compiles the revised surface; Phase 15 makes `src/` agree with it and then the fixture's restated halves can be deleted in favour of imports, one decision at a time. A restatement that outlives its implementation is how a fixture starts lying.

**Also here, because they are cheap once the settlement is open**: 13b B-2's ergonomics — `landing({ timing })` or `duration: number | (() => number)`, read at settle time inside the default runner so a consumer keeps the reduced-motion collapse, the retarget replay and the generation guard — and 13c N-2's probe annotation, which still describes the gap as unsolved.

**Done when.** The 05 §Authored-presentation acknowledgement matrix group passes — including the **synchronous-commit** row and the **stale acknowledgement across two operations** row, which are the two the Checkpoint C blockers turned into executable cases; the readiness and async-attempt rows pass against the new protocol; the reference integration is expressible without the consumer re-implementing supersede-and-never-drop; and **the whole existing suite is green against the revised SPI, with every Part I deviation re-checked rather than assumed** — Phase 14's carried obligation.

**Deviations and decisions recorded while implementing.**

- **The four `presentationReady` value-channel tests were deleted, not ported**, and the deletion is written up in `tests/COVERAGE.md` §Removed with the pre-revision protocol rather than left as a shrinking count. A rejecting promise, a hostile `then` accessor, a non-thenable value and the arm-time skip a synchronously-failing gate caused were all tests of a _value_ `holdForReadiness` no longer takes. Nothing regressed: the channel does not exist. What did go with them is the **rejection** capability, which the contract already recorded as a deliberate narrowing — latency only, bounded by a public option.
- **`armSettlement` lost a failure branch, which is a consequence rather than a simplification.** Readiness could previously fail _synchronously_, inside arm, because arm subscribed to a consumer thenable there; the branch existed to stop a runner being started for a settlement the checkpoint was about to abandon. A deadline cannot fail synchronously, so the branch is unreachable and gone. The landing half of arm is untouched, and `ARM_FAILED` still exists for it.
- **Every invalid acknowledgement is `DEV`-gated, and one message is shared by three arrival rows.** The duplicate report is a hoisted module-level function rather than three call sites, because the string is a constant either way and three of the five rows in the arrival table report the same event. The contradiction message is deliberately _different_ — it is what tells an integrator which half of the protocol they omitted.
- **The report assertions are the point of the duplicate rows, and the state assertions alone would not have been.** A gate released twice is invisible in the final DOM: the drop completes, the placeholder goes, the order is right. Every duplicate row therefore installs a landing runner that never completes, so the second gate makes the hold count observable, _and_ asserts the platform report. Asserting only the state passes against a kernel that swallows duplicates silently, which is the failure mode the kernel/behavior ownership split exists to prevent.
- **The cross-window row's first assertion was wrong the first time it was written, in the instructive direction.** It asserted the runner saw no `retarget`, on the reasoning that a correct kernel does nothing twice. But the _single_ release legitimately re-anchors and retargets — that is the queued action doing its job. What a second release would do is decrement the **landing** hold as well, reach zero and finalize a drop whose animation is still running. The row now pins that instead, and the comment says so, because the wrong version passed against three of the four mutations it was meant to catch.
- **13b B-2 shipped as `duration: number | (() => number)`, not `landing({ timing })`.** One option that takes either form beats two options that mean the same thing, and the thunk is resolved **once per landing** rather than once per `play` — a retarget replays the same trajectory budget instead of re-reading a thunk that may have moved on. Validation splits with the form: a number is range-checked at construction, a thunk's result at settle time, which is the earliest each can be checked at all.
- **The probes had to move, and the reason is worth recording.** Phase 14 wrote that the Phase 13 probes are "untouched and still import the pre-revision SPI, which is what makes them evidence". That was true while the revision was a document; the moment `src/` implements it, a probe importing `src/` either moves or stops compiling. Both were retimed rather than rewritten: the findings are unchanged, each assertion says which side of the revision it describes, and **13b N-1 and 13c N-2 still fail to compile for the opposite reason they used to** — no token is minted because C-3 declines to mint one, and no seam reports the rendered delta because D-35 records it in the lift session instead.
- **The fixture shrank by three restated blocks, and only three.** `SettlementScope`, `PreparedSettlement`, `SettlementTransition`, `KernelHost`, `LandingContext`/`Handle`/`Start`, `ResolutionOptions`, `ReorderResolution` and `SortableController` are now imported from `src/`. What stays restated is D-32, D-34 and D-35, which land with Phases 16 and 19–20 — deleting a restatement the moment its implementation arrives is the rule, and applying it early to the other three would recreate exactly the lie the rule prevents.
- **D-33 costs 70 bytes.** minimal 9.33 → **9.40 kB** brotli, complete 10.09 → **10.17 kB**, both inside the M-3 budgets with no budget change. That is the whole protocol: one boolean on the prepared gate plan, one host member, one controller method, one nullable runtime field, and the reports it replaced a promise subscription with.
- **`SortableRuntime`'s header comment was already right and had been wrong.** It said "seven mutable fields" while six existed; `pendingRequest` makes it seven. Noted rather than silently corrected, because the contract's own count (six before D-33, seven after) is what the module is checked against.

## Phase 16 — Keyboard sorting

**Landed 2026-08-07.** D-32 is implemented and the suite is green at **693 tests** (32 files). The 05 §Discrete input group is `tests/sortable/keyboard.browser.test.ts` (22 tests, public entrypoint) plus seven ingress and construction rows in `tests/kernel/kernel.browser.test.ts`. **Q-13 is answered: a multi-press mode is not required**, on parity or on user need, and the contract is not reopened — written up in [`.agents/docs/accessibility.md`](../../../.agents/docs/accessibility.md) §Drag, which is also the phase's accessibility review.

**Scope.** The discrete-input path, at parity with the ledger's keyboard rows.

**Deliverables.** D-32's `command` admission member and the pointerless lifecycle behind it — `ACTIVATE`, `RELEASE`, no pointer listeners, no capture; the one-slot command as a complete operation sharing the proposal protocol (the shipped package's constraint that request semantics cannot diverge between input modes is a requirement, not an implementation detail); the 05 §Discrete input matrix group; the `List` story's arrow-key hint restored; accessibility behavior checked against [`.agents/docs/accessibility.md`](../../../.agents/docs/accessibility.md).

**One thing to decide here rather than assume (05 §Q-13).** D-32 makes a command one slot, because that is what the shipped package does. If the accessibility review finds that a _multi-press_ mode — pick up, arrows, drop — is required for parity or for a real user need, that is a failing executable case and it reopens the contract; it must not be worked around by having the behavior fake a release.

**Where it does not go (ledger L-4).** `ArrowLeft` and `ArrowUp` are the _same_ command in the shipped package, and `ArrowRight` and `ArrowDown` are the same command. Keyboard reordering is therefore not axis-specific and must not live inside `vertical()` or any successor axis feature — which is also why Phase 17 inherits no keyboard question from this one. The shipped ingress is a second delegated listener on the same container, sharing `resolveSortablePress`, so `handle()` gates the keyboard path too; that sharing is parity, not incidental.

**Done when.** Keyboard and pointer reorders produce identical proposals for the same destination gap, asserted directly rather than inferred.

**Deviations and decisions recorded while implementing.**

- **One real defect, and the contract had already asserted the mechanism that closes it.** `action.prepare(COLLECTION)` short-circuited on `phase === PENDING`, with the comment "`PENDING` has no insertion to rebase". That was true for a press — home is seeded at activation — and D-32 broke it: a command commits `PENDING` with its destination already written. An `updateItems()` queued from inside the listener therefore carried a stale gap into release, which failed to build a proposal and produced `FAILURE_RELEASE`. **The fix is to test the insertion instead of the phase**, which is strictly more general, leaves the press path byte-identical, and is exactly what 02 §The command destination already claims when it says a command gap is "either rebased or the operation is cancelled before release ever runs". No command-specific revalidator was added, as the contract asks.
- **A command invalidated before `ACTIVATE` is abandoned silently, and that is the phase table, not an oversight.** The reconciliation stages a cancel, the cancel lands at `PENDING`, and `CANCEL at PENDING → retire` produces no terminal callback. No start was notified, so none is owed (I-31), and the observable — the user pressed an arrow and nothing happened — is the one an edge-item command already produces. The matrix row asserts the **absence of a classified failure**, because that is what the defect above produced.
- **`preventDefault()` moved to the kernel for the pointer path as well, which is a behavior change to code Phase 8a shipped.** C-03 specifies it for both modes; the reference `admit` had been calling it itself. Same observable result for the same events, one party responsible in both modes, and I-32 becomes enforceable rather than aspirational — with the tier-C residue stated: a behavior holds the `Event` and can still prevent a default the kernel would not have.

  **"Same observable result for the same events" is the clause D-46 falsifies, and 02 §"moving the call one frame outward produces the same observable result" is corrected with it.** The claim was true of _where the call is made_ and was never a claim about _whether it should be made at all_ — which is the question probe E asked and Checkpoint C did not. Eight of ten cases release-blocking, **six of them with no drag ever activating**, means the admission `preventDefault()` was destroying native focus, caret, selection and form-control behavior on presses that were never drags. Moving the call kept that intact by construction, and the honest reading of C-03 after Revision 2 is that it **relocated a defect without inspecting it**. The move is still right: the ingress owner performing the browser effect is what makes the withdrawal a one-line policy change in one party rather than a behavior-by-behavior audit.

- **The ingress boundary was generalized rather than duplicated.** `openIngress(admit)` is one latch across both listeners, which is what makes a `pointerdown` from inside `command.admit` and a `keydown` from inside `admit` refused by the same rule. The primary-press test stays _outside_ it, on the pointer listener: a secondary button must not open a transaction at all.
- **`armOperationInput` split into `armPointerInput` and `armCancelInput`.** A pointerless operation arms only the second, so `MOVE`, `UP` and `lostpointercapture` are structurally unreachable rather than defended by a `pointerId` comparison — I-33 as a property of the wiring rather than a guard someone has to remember. Pointer capture and its `root.isConnected` precondition are skipped for the same reason: with nothing to capture, the precondition guards nothing.
- **D-35 is not implemented and the pointerless landing origin is correct anyway.** 02 says D-32 "could not have been added correctly on its own" because the pre-D-35 origin is `pointerX - originX`, which for a command would be `(-originX, -originY)`. That is true for a behavior that seeds the pointer fields; this kernel mints a pointerless operation with **all four scalars at zero**, so the old formula yields `(0, 0)` — the same answer D-35 will give from the lift session. The dependency is real but already discharged, and the matrix row asserts `from === (0, 0)`, which holds on both sides of Phase 19–20. **Confirmed when D-35 landed** (D-76): the row passes against both computations, and the test says so rather than being counted as a falsifier.
- **Keyboard is a `BehaviorSpec` member, not an optional feature, and it costs every composition ~300 B.** minimal **9.33 → 9.90 kB**, complete **10.09 → 10.69 kB** (D-33's 70 B included). That is a deliberate accessibility position — a consumer cannot tree-shake away the second input mode — and the M-3 budgets were re-based together to say so, keeping the stated ~0.3 kB headroom. The alternative, a `keyboard()` feature, would have made an accessibility floor opt-in; it is recorded as rejected rather than unconsidered.
- **The story gained `role="list"`, `role="listitem"` and `tabIndex`, because the restored hint would otherwise be a lie.** `keydown` is bound on the container and must originate inside a row, so a row that cannot take focus cannot be reordered from the keyboard at all. The shipped package's `List` story has the same hint and the same missing `tabindex` — the inherited gap this phase found, not one drag2 introduced. Roles, focus order and announcements stay the consumer's, which is the correct boundary for a headless library and is now a `[Docs]` limitation rather than an undiscovered one.
- **Q-13 answered no, with reasons rather than by default.** Parity: the shipped package is one-slot and the ledger retains it. User need: one-press-per-move is a complete accessible pattern with no modal carrying state to get stuck in, and the grab-and-drop model's only advantage — several slots without intermediate commits — buys a mode with its own discoverability cost. Answered, not closed: a real need reopens the SPI legitimately under 00's rule.

---

## Phase 17 — two-dimensional insertion, the second axis

**Landed 2026-08-07.** The suite is green at **709 tests** (33 files). The 2-D rule ships as a **sibling axis feature** — `xy()` on `sortable/xy.js` — and the axis features are renamed to the axes they measure: `vertical()` → `y()` on `sortable/y.js`, with `x()` reserved for a future horizontal rule. The `Grid` story is restored and drives `xy()` explicitly.

**Scope.** The retained 2-D insertion capability, as first-party geometry.

**What the ledger found (L-8).** The shipped package has **no grid feature, because it has no axis concept at all**: `nearestSlot` is a squared-Euclidean search over both centre coordinates, `SortableOptions` has no `axis` member, and the shipped `Grid` story is the `List` story with different CSS. So 2-D is the shipped _default_, and drag2's `vertical()` is a **narrowing** of it. This phase removes a restriction drag2 introduced; it does not add one the shipped package had. That reframing does not reduce the work — the rule still has to be written against the frozen view types — but it does mean "grid support" is a parity row, and it is why the third shape below is the one with precedent.

**Why it is a phase and not a feature ticket.** It is the only test of a claim 03 makes structurally: that the insertion rule is pluggable. `vertical()` deliberately went one-dimensional, consuming a single frame field (`pointerY`), and dropped the shipped package's `compareDocumentPosition` call; a 2-D rule needs `pointerX` and that call back, and whether the consumer-declared view types (`InsertionFrameView` / `InsertionRuntimeView`, D-13/D-20) can express it without an import edge back to the behavior runtime is unknown. 8a already had to widen that view once.

**Its public decomposition is not decided here.** Whether this ships as a `grid()` sibling feature, as a parameterization of one axis feature, or as an unrestricted default that `vertical()` constrains — and which subpath carries it — follows the Phase 12 ledger's [row for 2-D sorting](ledger.md#5-sortable--two-dimensional-insertion) and the Phase 14 contract revision. Whichever is chosen must not make the 1-D case pay for the 2-D one: the minimal composition's 9.34 kB is a measured budget. Naming a subpath now would be the same class of error this part exists to correct: fixing a public shape ahead of the decision that owns it.

**Deliverables, which do not depend on that shape.**

- The 2-D insertion rule itself, and the shipped `Grid` story restored.
- The axis-exclusivity claim (duplicate axis feature rejected, the rejected contribution's private state cleaned) re-run with **two real axes** rather than one and a duplicate of itself.
- Whether the consumer-declared views expressed the 2-D rule unchanged, or needed a second widening — recorded either way, since 8a's widening is currently a single data point.
- Whatever export-topology change the decided shape implies, landed **in full before the modules exist** — the Phase 0 measurement precondition, for the same reason. If it adds a subpath, that reopens what "minimal" means, which 05 §Measurements — landed 2026-08-02 already names as an M-3 trigger.

**The decomposition, decided: a `grid()`-shaped sibling, named `xy()`.** The constraint decided it rather than taste. The option with shipped precedent — an unrestricted 2-D default that an axis feature narrows — **cannot** be built without breaking the stated rule, because a default lives in the behavior core and cannot be tree-shaken away: every list consumer would carry the 2-D metric _and_ the narrowing feature on top of it. One parameterized axis feature fails the same rule more cheaply (~120 B) but in the same direction. Two subpaths keep each composition paying for its own rule, and the packaging test asserts the absence in **both** directions rather than only the one that was easy to state.

**Deviations and decisions recorded while implementing.**

- **The axis features are renamed, and it is a breaking public change.** `vertical()` → `y()`, `sortable/vertical.js` → `sortable/y.js`; the 2-D rule is `xy()`; `x()` is reserved. `vertical` was a layout word for a rule that is about a coordinate, and the ambiguity only becomes real once a second axis exists. Recorded in 03 §The export topology this requires as the seventh changed cell — the second breaking change in Part II, after D-33's `ResolutionOptions`. **Part I's prose and the review files keep the old name**: they are provenance, and rewriting them would make the record lie about what was decided when.
- **The shared cache costs the list composition 60 B, and it is recorded rather than absorbed.** `rect-index.ts` is dimension-neutral — it already packed both centres — and both rules hold one privately. The alternative was two copies of a geometry cache that must stay in step, where a divergence is a _silent correctness bug_ and not a style one. The 2-D rule itself still costs a list consumer nothing, which is the constraint the shape decision was made under. minimal **9.90 → 9.96 kB**; `minimal (xy)` measures **10.01 kB** as a peer rather than being assumed equal.
- **`RectIndex` exposes fields, not accessors, and the first version did not.** `values()` / `count()` as methods cost **90 B** on the minimal composition and two calls per resolution on the hot path, for encapsulation nothing can observe — the whole record is private to one feature instance. Caught by re-measuring rather than by review.
- **The frame view was widened a second time, and D-13's mechanism held.** 8a added `item`; this adds `pointerX`. Both were additive, both satisfied structurally by the behavior's existing frame with no wrapper, no allocation and no import edge back to the runtime. The honest reading of two data points is that the view is a **growing structural contract**, not a fixed one — recorded in `slots.ts` and in `xy.ts`, since Phase 17 was asked for exactly this evidence and a single data point could not have given it.
- **`y()` is not `xy()` with an axis switched off, and the suite says so.** A single-column list is where a 2-D rule is _nearly_ right: carry the pointer horizontally outside the column and every candidate's X term grows by the same amount, but the squared sum lets that shared term swamp the Y ordering near a boundary. The composed suite pins the converse directly — the sideways drag that `xy()` reorders, `y()` proposes nothing for — so the split is a capability difference rather than a preference.
- **The composed 2-D fixture must use a flow layout, and finding that out was the phase's one real debugging session.** With absolutely-positioned cells the placeholder is inert: moving it in the document moves nothing on screen, the incumbent's centre never catches up with the pointer, and the rule oscillates between two gaps on successive resolutions — which surfaced as a drag that completed as a **no-op**. That is not a defect in the rule; it is what placeholder-as-incumbent hysteresis _is_, and it is only observable once the placeholder can reflow. Written up in `tests/COVERAGE.md`, because the next person to write a 2-D fixture will hit it.
- **Axis exclusivity is now tested with two real modules.** It had been one feature against a duplicate of itself, which cannot tell an exclusivity rule apart from an idempotence bug. Both orders are asserted, and the cleanup row checks that a later valid composition still assembles.

---

## Checkpoint D — the sortable behavior is complete

**What exists.** Pointer **and** keyboard input; one-dimensional **and** two-dimensional insertion; the full optional feature set; the new settlement protocol. Every sortable row in the ledger closed or explicitly declined.

**Read this before the C2/C3/C4/C5 bullets below: D-37 (Revision 2) retires the proof domain they were built for, and retires none of their fixes.** Five review passes here narrowed one obligation — I-36's indirect-invocation clause — from a quantifier over call sites, to provisioning plus a five-act floor plus a register of named ceilings, to bimodal provisioning plus a two-section register, and finally to a 62-stretch sweep of `src/sortable/`. The clause's own subject was "every DOM method the library calls on a consumer-owned node", which C4-01 and ledger L-12 both state **does not terminate as a list**. D-37 replaces it with a finite, API-shaped domain: after logical closure the library invokes no **declared consumer slot**, admits no operation, and publishes no lifecycle or domain event; internal work already inside the current transaction may finish provided it survives the boundary in no observable form. **Every reading these passes landed stays landed** — the floor is preserved verbatim and D-37 licenses removing nothing — and every defect they found still breaches it. What goes is the **ceiling register** and the **stretch table as a normative enumeration**, together with the obligation to reproduce either for a second behavior. The bullets are kept in full: they are the record of what it costs to chase an undischargeable quantifier through five passes, and that is the most transferable thing in this checkpoint.

**What to review.** The behavior against the ledger, and the feature model under a load it has not carried: two axes, two input modes.

**Exit.** No open sortable parity item. This is the last checkpoint before the kernel acquires a second behavior, and therefore the last cheap moment to change anything sortable-shaped that leaked into the kernel.

**Review 1 — [`reviews/checkpoint-d-1.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-1.md), eight findings, all resolved.** D2 and D5 closed first, in [`reviews/checkpoint-d-1-resolution-d2-d5.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-1-resolution-d2-d5.md); D1, D3, D4, D6, D7 and D8 in [`reviews/checkpoint-d-1-resolution-d1-d3-d4-d6-d7-d8.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-1-resolution-d1-d3-d4-d6-d7-d8.md). One finding, **L-11**, is deferred to Phase 23 by the owner's decision with the work attached there.

**Deviations and decisions recorded while implementing.**

- **Three of the four behavior defects were drift, not decisions, and none had a document behind it.** D1's double `getHandle`, D3's pre-latch validation and D4's skipped duration thunk each contradicted something already written down — the shared-admission-rule row in the ledger, the `updateItems` no-op promise, and `landing({ duration })`'s own documented call timing. None was recorded anywhere as deliberate. They are fixed as regressions against the existing specification rather than reclassified, and each is pinned by a test that **fails against the pre-fix source** — verified by reverting each fix in turn, not assumed.
- **D6 is closed as _retain, implementation corrected_, and the search for a contrary decision is the finding.** The ledger retains the shipped `{ duration: 200, easing: 'ease' }`; `landing.ts` shipped `'ease-out'`. Nothing in the plan, the probes, the measurements or the contract records a deliberate redesign, so there was no decision to preserve — only drift, in a default that changes the motion of every consumer who installs `landing()` without an easing. `layoutAnimation()`'s own `'ease-out'` default is deliberately left alone: it has no shipped counterpart and therefore no parity constraint, and unifying the two would be a redesign nobody asked for.
- **D3's terminal check is on `updateItems` only, and the asymmetry is the decision.** `cancel` and `destroy` _are_ the kernel's own members spread through unchanged, and the kernel's closed latch already makes both inert before they do any work — a second latch in front of them would be duplicated state that can disagree. ~~`ready()` deliberately keeps reporting: a post-`destroy()` acknowledgement is stale by definition, and naming it is exactly what that `DEV` report is for.~~ The latch lives on the controller rather than being read from the kernel because `KernelHost` does not expose `closed`, and widening a frozen SPI type for a controller's private bookkeeping is the change 00 forbids without a failing executable case.

  **Carried forward through Revision 2, with both of its subjects replaced.** `updateItems` becomes `invalidate()` (D-44) and inherits the check for a sharper reason than the original had: the member's whole job is to make the library re-read the consumer's `items` thunk, so a post-`destroy()` invalidation would call consumer code from a closed controller — exactly the class D-37 names as irreducible. `ready()` is deleted with the rest of D-33 (D-41), so the asymmetry now has two members on one side and two on the other. **The reasoning is unchanged and so is the latch**; D-36 reinforces it, because the logical latch is set on `destroy()`'s closing statement while physical teardown may defer, and D-38 forbids reading physical state for this question — so the controller's own logical latch is the only correct source, not any resource the deferred teardown will abort.

- **D3's sharpest case is not the direct call.** A post-`destroy()` duplicate thrown from a consumer callback does not merely reach the caller: `onStart` runs inside `activation.effect`, so the `TypeError` was classified `FAILURE_ACTIVATION` against an operation the consumer had already destroyed. The regression pins that arrival as well as the direct one.
- **D7 advanced the live normative sections and deliberately left history alone.** Contract 03's feature list, axis sections, diagnostics table, private-state table, minimal fixture and M-3 table now name `y()`/`xy()`; the ledger's §5 deferral is closed with the decided shape and its reason. Part I prose, the probe write-ups and the review files keep `vertical()` as provenance. The rule applied: only documents declared as current authorities have to stop giving two executable readings.
- **One row of the contract's own tree-shaking criterion was withdrawn rather than restated.** "Any input mode other than pointer must be absent from the minimal build" predates D-32 and is contradicted by the artifact: keyboard is a `BehaviorSpec` member, so every composition carries it and no consumer can shake it away. That was Phase 16's deliberate accessibility position, but the criterion it invalidated was never updated — leaving it standing made the minimal build's own packaging test unpassable in principle. Withdrawn with the reason attached, not quietly deleted.
- **The ledger named the wrong failure stage for admission-resolver throws.** It said `FAILURE_ACTIVATION`; the kernel and the test matrix both use `FAILURE_ADMISSION`, which is a different stage with a different recovery and belongs to the phase after admission. The ledger was the only document with it wrong, which is why nothing failed on it.
- **Checkpoint D's fixes cost 40 B.** minimal 9.97 → **10.01 kB**, `minimal (xy)` 10.04 → **10.05 kB**, complete 10.78 → **10.82 kB**; module counts unchanged, every composition inside its budget. The README and contract 03 size tables had both been left at the pre-keyboard four-composition figures and are re-measured rather than recopied — the harness has five compositions now.
- **Test count 713 → 724, with nothing removed or weakened.** **Four** for D1, three for D3, **four** for D4/D6 — corrected at review 2 (C2-05), which recounted the eleven; the earlier 5/3/3 split did not add up. The existing reduced-motion and handle-gating tests are kept and the reduced-motion stub hoisted into one helper they share.

**Review 2 — [`reviews/checkpoint-d-2.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-2.md), five findings, all resolved.** C2-01 was decided by the architect in [`reviews/checkpoint-d-2-resolution-c2-01.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-2-resolution-c2-01.md) and implemented as specified; the implementation record for all five is [`reviews/checkpoint-d-2-resolution-implementation.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-2-resolution-implementation.md).

- **C2-01 — the terminal barrier stopped at the seam boundary, and the fix is behavior-owned.** A consumer resolver invoked in a sequence could call `controller.destroy()` and the sequence carried on: `getVisual` for every later candidate inside the axis cache rebuild, `getVisual` after a `handle()` resolver at admission, and — unreported by the review — the eager rebuild and every `afterMove` hook after a placeholder custom element's reaction inside a committed `movePlaceholder`. The candidate loop additionally **resurrected the cache `retire()` had just emptied**, marking it clean and re-pinning every row of a destroyed controller against I-20, which is why the fix restores the retired state rather than `break`ing. Closed as **I-36** (tier C, non-promotable) and **F-47**; the third review (C3-03) completed the pairing by re-tiering **I-6** itself, which C2-01 had left at an unqualified B — with the latch moved from `createSortableController`'s closure to `SortableRuntime.closed` — one latch, five readers — and the one site that cannot reach the runtime receiving it as `live()` on the per-operation view, the **fourth** additive widening of the D-13 consumer-declared view. **The frozen SPI did not reopen**: contract 00's bar has two conjuncts and this case fails the second, because the frozen SPI expresses the fix completely. The existing `activation.effect` guard keeps reading `presentation.signal.aborted`, which is strictly stronger there; `release.prepare` deliberately takes no guard, because the kernel already stages nothing for an invalidated preparation. Ledger **L-12**, which is explicitly _not_ a parity row: shipped `rebuildRectIndex` has no liveness check either.
- **C2-01's cost, measured after the fact.** minimal 10.01 → **10.07 kB** (+60 B), `minimal (xy)` 10.05 → **10.12 kB** (+70 B), `+ layoutAnimation` 10.42 → **10.51 kB** (+90 B), `+ landing` 10.29 → **10.36 kB** (+70 B), complete 10.82 → **10.85 kB** (+30 B), baseline A 10.54 → **10.60 kB**. Every composition still inside its budget; module counts unchanged. The `+ layoutAnimation` figure came in 20 B above the architect's predicted band, which is inside brotli's noise for a single-composition delta and did not change any decision.
- **One guard is specified but not independently observable, and it is kept anyway.** `action.prepare(TAG_SPATIAL)`'s `rt.closed` test after `resolveInsertion` is unreachable through any first-party composition: the loop barrier already makes a destroyed traversal resolve to `null`, so the `resolved === null` disjunct always fires first. It is defence in depth against a future axis rule that reaches consumer code by another route, it costs nothing measurable, and it is recorded here rather than removed so the next reviewer does not read it as dead code that slipped through. `action.effect`'s bracket guard had the same problem against `measureInSeam`'s guard, and is pinned by composing an axis feature with **no eager `measure`** — a shape the contract explicitly supports.
- **C2-02 — the public landing contract still specified the old API.** The implementation had `duration?: number | (() => number)` since Phase 15 and validated a thunk once at settlement, while contract 03 published `duration?: number` and said every option is validated at construction, the README repeated it, and the ledger gave both readings at once. The signature and the domain text now distinguish **fixed durations, validated at construction** from **thunks, invoked and validated once per landing**; the ledger's retained-behavior row no longer opens with "fixes timing at construction", and L-6 no longer calls the option a future Phase 15-or-22 change it made in Phase 15.
- **C2-03 — two loss rows were factually wrong, and the decision stands.** D5's frozen surface is untouched. `AnimationTiming` **is** a real named export of shipped `sortable.ts`, so "nothing nameable" was wrong and the row is reclassified _redesign_ → **drop** in both places, with the loss stated; its "strictly wider" rationale is withdrawn as false — DOM `EffectTiming.duration` and drag2's duration domain overlap and neither contains the other, and the decision never needed the claim. Shipped `CancellationReason` is not a named export but **is** structurally exposed through the exported `SortableCancelResult`, so a shipped consumer could switch on `reason.type` without importing anything; "nothing against shipped" was wrong even though the redesign remains preferable. The four deliberate omissions now carry negative assertions in the consumer type fixture, which is what they lacked.
- **C2-04 — normatively-ranked documents still published the removed API in present tense, and the fix advances them rather than weakening the ranking.** Contract 00 ranks 00–04 normative in precedence order, so D7's "remaining Part I `vertical()` prose is provenance" did not hold for a current declaration, a consumer-facing example and a feature-state table. Every _current_ statement in 00–04 now names `y()`/`xy()`. The alternative — relabelling parts of 00–04 as historical — was rejected because the precedence ranking is itself normative and load-bearing for every other document, and a contract that has to tell you which of its paragraphs are true is worse than one that is true. Contract 03's remaining `vertical()` uses are all narrative about an earlier draft or an earlier probe and are now explicitly framed as such in one place; `plan.md` and the review files stay provenance and are untouched.
- **C2-05 — five verifiable residues, all corrected.** `readinessTimeout` added to contract 03's `SortableCallbacks` listing; eight TypeDoc entrypoints → nine; the "deltas unchanged since M-3" claim withdrawn and the numbers re-measured **after** C2-01 rather than carried forward; both axis module headers now say candidate _visual_; and the eleven-test attribution corrected above.

**Review 3 — [`reviews/checkpoint-d-3.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-3.md), four findings, all resolved.** Checkpoint D review 3 (C3-03) was decided by the architect in [`reviews/checkpoint-d-3-resolution-c3-03.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-3-resolution-c3-03.md) and applied as specified; the implementation record for all four is [`reviews/checkpoint-d-3-resolution-implementation.md`](reviews/checkpoint-d/checkpoint-d-3-resolution-implementation.md). **The IDs collide with Checkpoint C's third review**, whose unrelated readiness-acknowledgement items are also C3-01…C3-04 (§Phase 13b, §Phase 14, and 05's test matrix); every reference below is Checkpoint **D** review 3.

- **Checkpoint D review 3 (C3-01) — an aborted candidate rebuild still read placeholder geometry after `destroy()`, and the fix is a return channel.** C2-01 stopped the resolver calls but `RectIndex.refresh` still returned `void`, so both axes continued straight past an aborted rebuild into `centreOf(placeholder)` / `placeholder.getBoundingClientRect()`. The placeholder is **consumer-owned** and may override `getBoundingClientRect()`, so that is an observable post-destroy consumer callback, not wasted layout work — which is exactly the indirect-invocation clause C3-03 added to I-36. **`refresh` now returns `boolean`**, `false` only on an abort, and each axis returns `null` on it. This overrides C2-01 §4A's "no new return channel is needed", which was true only of the _count_ path it was reasoning about: `count === 0` does make the scan find nothing, but the placeholder read happens **before** the scan. Chosen over a per-axis `runtime.live()` recheck on the repo's stated priority order — the recheck costs an indirect call per resolution in **every** composition, including the minimal one that composes no `visual()` and can therefore never abort, while the return channel is free on the hot path; it is also one shared obligation rather than one a future third axis can forget. Two mirrored regressions, one per axis, instrument the placeholder's `getBoundingClientRect` and were verified to fail against pre-fix source (expected 0, received 1). The two existing cases claimed "no geometry is read after it" while asserting only the resolver list; the claim is narrowed to what each actually checks and the missing half is a case of its own.
- **Checkpoint D review 3 (C3-02) — C2-04's terminology sweep completed.** Thirteen further present-tense declarations advanced to axis-neutral wording: D-34 in 00; `rollback`, the tier-C vacuity note, the `Activation` default, the seam-by-seam heading, hit testing, the landing origin, the action-tag count and the failure-stage list in 02; the M-3 baseline sentence in 03; and **four the review did not name**, all in 05 — I-17's mechanism cell, its note, Q-4's tag count and Q-7's duplicate-read problem. C2-04's rule is unchanged and was simply not applied everywhere; 03's completion note now says so.
